    - **The rule, and why it is not symmetric**: risk causes **may** overwrite each other (a later interception is newer evidence, and OPT-029's cause-scoped release depends on that); a **non-risk** cause may only overwrite when it actually extended the window. The asymmetry follows from what each side means: a risk cause is an observation about the account, a non-risk cause is an observation about *us* (our budget, our timeout, our socket). Our own degradation decision must not erase what the platform told us.
    - **The comparison reads `_effective_cooldown_severity`, not the raw record** — the release path uses that function, so both sites must ask the same question. When the record is missing it degrades to the ledger's most recent interception, and the failure direction is deliberately "keep the risk cause": better to leave a human exit open than to silently close one.
    - `_RISK_CAUSES` (`auth`/`ratelimit`/`waf`) is the single definition of "risk cause"; `timeout`, `truncated` and `unconfigured` are upstream faults or configuration gaps and sit outside it by construction.
    - Pinned by `tests/test_budget_capped_evidence.py::test_a_non_risk_escalation_never_relabels_a_live_risk_cause` (suite now 8 cases), which asserts both halves: the risk cause survives a budget-capped escalation **and** still releases on credential renewal, while the risk→risk overwrite (waf → ratelimit) is asserted as an explicit control so the rule cannot be over-generalised into "causes never change".

61. **Codex CLI Ignores `OPENAI_BASE_URL` — the quick-start sent prompts to OpenAI (`README.md`, `scripts/e2e/`)**:
    - **[E2E] The documented integration was not an integration at all.** The README's Codex section told users to run `OPENAI_BASE_URL=http://127.0.0.1:8000/v1 OPENAI_API_KEY=test-key codex -m gpt-4o "…"`. Measured against the real `codex` 0.145.0 binary: with **only** that variable set, its `ModelProviderInfo` reports `base_url: None` and the CLI opens `wss://api.openai.com/v1/responses` (plus `https://chatgpt.com/backend-api/plugins/featured`) — the user's prompts and code never reached the gateway. The binary contains **zero** occurrences of the string `OPENAI_BASE_URL`; it knows only `OPENAI_API_KEY` / `OPENAI_ORGANIZATION` / `OPENAI_PROJECT`. A custom provider must be declared in `$CODEX_HOME/config.toml` under `[model_providers.<id>]` with `base_url` + `wire_api = "responses"` + `requires_openai_auth = false`.
    - **Why this is the worst possible symptom**: it fails *silently and successfully*. No error, no gateway log line, no diagnostic — the user sees a normal answer produced by a different service, while the local models they configured are never called. Every avoidance mechanism in this file (OPT-011…035) is scoped to "which upstream did we touch", so a setup that touches none of them reports a perfectly clean risk posture. This is the same defect class as OPT-035 ("the dossier says the node is dispatchable") one layer out: **the documentation asserted a behaviour the client does not have**.
    - **The verification instrument had the same bug.** A harness that drove `codex exec` with `OPENAI_BASE_URL` would have measured OpenAI, not the gateway — so the fix is not just a doc edit. `scripts/e2e/run_agent_loop.py` now writes a real `CODEX_HOME/config.toml` per run and asserts the run closed a two-round tool loop, which is unachievable against a foreign service. **The lesson generalises: a test that does not prove the client reached *this* process proves nothing about this process.**
    - **The harness itself is now in the repository, not in `/tmp`.** Every `tests/test_*.py` suite is offline and asserts on the gateway's own objects; those suites stay green even if the client rejects every byte the gateway sends. `scripts/e2e/scripted_upstream.py` + `scripts/e2e/run_agent_loop.py` make the **real binary** the judge of the one question the project exists to answer, and they assert the loop on three independent pieces of evidence: the marker file the client really created (not the gateway's claim), at least two upstream bodies (a tool result came back), and the captured prompt really carried both the tool contract and `[Tool Result for <id>]`.
    - **Both agent CLIs verified green, measured — this is the product's acceptance criterion, not a demo:**
      ```
      claude: PASS (exit=0, bodies=11, tool_ran=True, tool=Bash,         tool_markers=10/10)  # 2.1.293
      codex:  PASS (exit=0, bodies=11, tool_ran=True, tool=exec_command, tool_markers=10/10)  # 0.145.0

      # --parallel --rounds 10 : both binaries concurrently, ONE gateway, 22 bodies total
      claude: PASS (bodies=11, tool_markers=10/10, tool=Bash)
      codex:  PASS (bodies=11, tool_markers=10/10, tool=exec_command)
      ```
      Both arms also pass at 2 and 5 rounds. In every case **one marker file per round** was created by the real binary, so the proof is "round *n* really executed", not "some round did"; and the final answer came only after the client had seen every `[Tool Result for <id>]` in the captured prompt.
    - **`--rounds` exists because two rounds prove the loop closes, not that it *stays* closed.** A two-request loop only exercises the first history replay. Real agent sessions run 5–15 calls, and each round re-sends the whole accumulated history through the protocol handlers — so an item-indexing defect (section 32b), a history-replay defect (sections 43/45) or a stop-truncation defect (section 35) can be invisible at round 2 and fatal at round 5. Driving 10 rounds with both real binaries is the cheapest instrument that covers the shape the product is actually used in.
    - **Two harness traps in the multi-round path, both measured.** The per-round marker command must be **appended** to the base one (`base && touch ROUND_n`); an earlier version replaced it, and every multi-round run reported "the client never executed the tool" while all five `ROUND_*` markers sat on disk — a harness defect that reads exactly like a product defect. And each client needs its **own** working directory, or "round 3 of claude" is satisfiable by "round 3 of codex".
    - **A third trap, found by the first `--rounds 3` run after the refactor: the harness disagreed with itself about what a round marker is.** `scripted_upstream._command_for()` emitted the bare base command for round one (only rounds >1 wrote `ROUND_n`), while the runner required the full set `ROUND_1..N`. Both real binaries therefore reported `tool_markers=2/3 ... missing ['ROUND_1']` on a 3-round run that had executed perfectly — `bodies=4`, `tool_ran=True`, `round2_saw_result=True`, every round served. The correct reading of that output is "the run is green and the marker contract has two definitions", not "the loop stopped early". Fixed at the source: in multi-round mode **every** round writes its marker, so the runner's set is the upstream's set by construction. This is the same defect shape as OPT-032/035 ([M1]'s "an assertion about a window that is not the window the code protects") moved into the test harness: **a harness whose two halves disagree reports the disagreement as a product failure.**
    - **`--parallel` is the setup developers actually run and nothing else in the repo exercised**: two agents, two terminals, one gateway. Both real binaries drive the *same* process concurrently for N rounds each, so their requests interleave through one connection pool, one set of per-node semaphores, one singleflight manager and one session-affinity registry. Measured (`--parallel --rounds 10`, one gateway): `claude` 11 bodies / 10/10 round markers, `codex` 11 bodies / 10/10, **22 upstream bodies total**, both `EXIT=0`, no round lost. **Attribution is the design problem**: once both clients share one upstream log, "which client ran this round" cannot be read from the client — it is read from the **tool each request declared** (`Bash` ⇒ claude, `exec_command` ⇒ codex) in `scripted_upstream._client_tag()`, with marker paths derived the same way and a separate working directory per client. Without that, both clients write into one directory and "claude ran 10 rounds" is satisfiable by codex's rounds: a green run measuring nothing.
    - **The Codex arm was red for a while and the failure was not where it looked.** It failed with `name '_collect_reclaim_task' is not defined` — a half-landed refactor in `providers/failover.py` where the upstream stream's `finally` referenced a task name that existed nowhere in the repo (`grep` hit only that one line). Every streaming request died at the reclaim seam, so no `[Tool Result]` could ever come back and the loop could not close. The durable fix is `_close_raw_stream()`: it dispatches the close as an **independent task** and shields the wait, so the upstream is closed *before* `_release_sem()` even on the cancellation path. Verified with counters on the real HTTP path: `opened == closed` per node, semaphores returned value-for-value, `active_concurrency == 0`, across all three protocols. **The lesson: a resource-release path that only runs on the disconnect branch is invisible to any test that never disconnects — the E2E binary was the instrument that surfaced it.**
    - **Three harness traps, each of which produced a false reading before it was fixed** (all recorded in CLAUDE.md section 60 for the earlier `/tmp` version, and now encoded in the script): a `pkill -f` in the same compound Bash command kills its own process group and returns 144, so the `nohup` half never runs and the next `curl` gets 000; the round detector must match a **real** id (`\[Tool Result for [A-Za-z0-9_-]+`) because the gateway's own tool-contract text contains the literal `[Tool Result for <id>]`; and the tool must be chosen from the names the client **declared** (picking the first declared name selects `Agent` on Claude Code, which spawns a subagent instead of running the marker command — the loop "closes" while measuring the wrong thing). The script now prefers a configured shell-tool order (`Bash`, `exec_command`, …) but only among declared names.

62. **A Namespace Container Is Not a Callable Tool (`providers/anthropic_compat.py:flatten_namespace_tools`)**:
    - **[TOOL-LOOP] The declaration the gateway rendered as `Args: {}` with the wrong name.** Captured verbatim from real `codex exec` (0.145.0) into `tests/fixtures/codex_multi_agent_v1_namespace.json`: alongside the nine named tools, Codex declares `{"type":"namespace","name":"multi_agent_v1","description":"Tools for spawning and managing sub-agents.","tools":[close_agent, resume_agent, send_input, spawn_agent, wait_agent]}`. It is a **container of five callable function sub-tools**, not a tool. The generic branch took the top-level `name`/`description`, found no `parameters`, and emitted `` - `multi_agent_v1`: … `` + `Args: {}`. Two errors at once: all five sub-tool names and every parameter schema vanished (the model could not call them), and the model was told the callable name is `multi_agent_v1`.
    - **Client routing measured, changing only the emitted call item against the same mock upstream** — and the conclusion below was *corrected after re-measuring*, because the first reading inverted the export side. The judge has to be **whether the tool really executed** (`collab: SpawnAgent` and a real `{"agent_id":…}` in the `function_call_output`), never the model's own wording: in every arm the mock model still says "Sub-agent spawned.", so reply text cannot discriminate.

      | emitted item | client result |
      |---|---|
      | `exec_command`, no `namespace` | ran (`hi`) |
      | `exec_command` + `namespace` | `unsupported call: multi_agent_v1exec_command` |
      | `spawn_agent`, no `namespace` | `unsupported call: spawn_agent` |
      | `spawn_agent` + `"bogus_ns"` | `unsupported call: bogus_nsspawn_agent` |
      | `spawn_agent` + the real `namespace` | `collab: SpawnAgent` → `{"agent_id":…}` |
      | `multi_agent_v1.spawn_agent` / `::` / `__` | `unsupported call` |

      The table lookup key is `namespace + name` **concatenated with no separator**, which produces two opposite obligations: a namespaced sub-tool **must** carry its `namespace` on the wire, and an ordinary tool **must not**. "Bare name + full schema" is the correct shape for the **declaration** side only (prompt and parse allowlist); the **export** side has to put the namespace back. Verified green against the real emitter with a real `codex exec` (`requests=3`, `unsupported=0`, sub-agent id returned), and identically under both Codex feature sets — with `multi_agent_v2` off the declared container is `multi_agent_v1` (5 sub-tools), with it on it is `collaboration` (6); the name is client-declared either way, so the gateway must never hardcode it. An earlier version of this entry reported bare `spawn_agent` as routed; that reading came from a harness whose monkeypatch used a **positional** `status` against a keyword-only signature, so all five arms crashed identically and the crash was mistaken for a client rejection.
    - **One definition of "which namespace owns this tool"**: `namespace_of_tools()` derives `tool -> namespace` from the *output of the same* `flatten_namespace_tools` call that feeds the prompt and the allowlist, so the three views cannot drift. `function_call_item(..., namespace=...)` is the only place the field is set, and both streaming and non-streaming `/v1/responses` paths read the map from one call site each.
    - **Flattening must cover the prompt *and* the parse allowlist, or it fixes nothing.** The prompt decides what the model tries to call; `resolve_allowed_tools` decides what may be returned. Flattening only the prompt makes the model write `spawn_agent` and have it discarded as undeclared — the identical symptom ("the tool did not run"), one layer harder to find. `flatten_namespace_tools` (depth-capped at 4, non-mutating, order-preserving for ordinary declarations) is the single implementation, applied at each request model's `tools` validator so no path can see two interpretations.
    - **The namespace's own name is still information**: it moves to its own `## Tool Namespaces (not callable)` section rather than being rendered as a callable bullet, and deliberately separate from the nameless-builtin section — the causes differ (that one has no name; this one has a name that must not be called), and the model's next action is decided by the cause.
    - **The OpenAI chat path was missed by the first round and closed here**: `ChatCompletionRequest` lives in `server.py`, so the prompt advertised `spawn_agent` while the allowlist still held only `multi_agent_v1`. Unreachable from real Codex (it only uses `/v1/responses`) but a public protocol entry point; it now carries the same validator. Verified before the fix: `PROMPT declares spawn_agent: True` / `ALLOWLIST: ['exec_command', 'multi_agent_v1']`.
    - **Zero regression**: ordinary declarations pass through byte-identically and in order; the real 10-entry capture yields 13 callable tools with `spawn_agent`'s five optional properties and `send_input.items`' nested object array intact, and the `(None, 300)` degradation tier still fits.
    - Pinned by 6 cases (5 in `tests/test_responses_custom_tools.py`, 1 in `tests/test_tool_schema.py`), verified red pre-fix with `namespace 仍被渲染成一个可调用工具，且参数是空的 Args: {}`. One of the new assertions was itself too broad on first write — `"- \`multi_agent_v1\`" not in prompt` also matched the intentional "not callable" note line, so the judgement has to be made on the **callable bullet shape** (`- \`name\`:` + `Args:`), not on the substring. The export-side rule has its own case, `test_namespaced_sub_tool_call_carries_its_namespace_on_the_wire`, which pins **both** directions (sub-tool carries the namespace, ordinary tool carries none) plus the namespace container never becoming a callable name — asserting the map by reading `namespace_of_tools` alone would have passed while the emitter still dropped the field.

63. **Readiness Surface Must Not Report Success (`server.py:health`, `tests/test_health_truthfulness.py`)**:
    - **[HEALTH-TRUTH] The third place claiming a capability the dispatcher does not have.** `GET /health` returned `"status": "ok"` unconditionally. Measured on a fresh clone (`session/` empty — which is this machine's state too): HTTP 200, `status: ok`, all five `*_configured: false`, all five providers `offline`. The gateway could serve **nothing**, and its readiness endpoint said it was ready.
    - **Why this one matters more than the other two**: OPT-032 (`audit_dispatch`) and OPT-035 (dossier) lied to a human who had already gone looking for the answer. `/health` is the *first* thing every operator, monitoring probe and agent setup script asks — `ok` is an instruction to start sending requests. Every one of them then ends in 401, and nothing in the response points at the cause (no credentials), so the operator debugs the wrong thing. The upstream account is untouched throughout, so the risk posture is clean while the deployment is unusable: the same "the system asserts something the code does not do" shape, on the surface with the widest audience.
    - **One reading, again**: `dispatchable` per node is `configured and evaluate_dispatch_gate(pk)["dispatchable"]` — the *same* gate the dispatcher asks, plus the one bit the gate deliberately does not express (the gate answers about known-but-blocked nodes; "has no credential at all" is a dispatch-boundary fact, not a risk state). No second definition was introduced. `status` is `ok` iff at least one node is dispatchable, else `degraded` with a `status_reason` that names the remedy (`login.py all`) or, when credentials exist but every node is blocked, the blocking causes.
    - **Availability semantics preserved**: short soft coolings are demote-only (OPT-021's last-resort), so the gate answers `dispatchable: True` for them and `/health` keeps saying `ok`. The change is scoped to "this deployment cannot serve at all", not "this deployment is not at its best".
    - **Asserted as a property, not as scenarios**: `test_health_dispatchable_agrees_with_the_single_gate` checks `dispatchable == configured and gate.dispatchable` for **every node × every state** (healthy / no-credentials / disabled), because the drift defects in this file were each one missed cell. Two more cases pin the directions a naive fix gets wrong: no credentials ⇒ `degraded` **with** a non-empty reason, and one healthy node ⇒ still `ok` (a health surface that is permanently degraded is as useless as one that is permanently ok).
    - `tests/test_healing.py`'s pre-existing `assert data["status"] == "ok"` pinned exactly this defect on this machine (empty `session/`); it was corrected to the new contract with the reason recorded inline, the same treatment §45 gave the two stale `test_server.py` cases.
64. **An Unconfigured Node Is Not an Intercepted One (`providers/failover.py:record_failure`, `providers/credential_renewal.py:note_unconfigured`, `session_store.py:is_configured`)**:
    - **[DAY-ZERO] The state every new user hits first, and the heaviest avoidance tier was applied to it.** After `git clone`, `session/` is empty but the gateway starts. `server.py:_make_provider` then raises `ProviderError("未检测到 Qwen 令牌。…", status=401, err_type="auth_error")` — a purely **local** conclusion, zero upstream calls — and `execute_chat`'s `except` reads only `exc.status`, so it reached `record_failure` as an upstream HTTP 401. Measured on a real `CooldownTracker` with an isolated state dir: one request per node → `cooldown = {qwen: 1799.9, deepseek: 1800.0, doubao: 1800.0, kimi: 1800.0, glm: 1800.0}`. A real `claude -p` issues 7 requests in 40 s (its own retry loop), so **one agent start-up** puts all five nodes into 30-minute auth avoidance — with zero upstream calls and zero interception.
    - **The worse half is a self-lock, and it is the OPT-026 defect wearing a new trigger.** `CredentialRenewalTracker.note_auth_failure()` returns early with **no record at all** when `credential_fingerprint()` is `None` (no file to fingerprint), and `renewed_providers()` only scans *existing* records. So the release path has no baseline to compare against: after the operator runs the `login.py` the gateway itself told them to run, `release_renewed_credentials()` returns `[]` and the cooldown is untouched. The gateway's own remedy is inert, measured exactly as `released=[], cooldown={'qwen': 59.9, …}` in the fixed short-cooling state and `1800.0` in the pre-fix state.
    - **Detection is by fact, not by message.** The predicate is `session_store.is_configured(pk)` — "does this provider have a credential *right now*" — deliberately the same condition `_make_provider` raises on and `healing/monitor.py:334` already judges (`if not sess or not sess.token: return False, f"{name} 未配置有效令牌"`). Sniffing the error text would have been the easier fix and the wrong one: the suite drives `record_failure("qwen", 401, <the real message>)` straight through, and any other caller shape (a different provider's wording, a re-worded message) must not decide whether an account is booked as banned.
    - **Four properties, and each is load-bearing on its own**: (a) **not a risk interception** — no ledger append, no `credential_watch.note_auth_failure` (five unconfigured nodes would otherwise trip the OPT-025 ring-wide outage detector and fast-fail the whole ring on a fresh clone), no probation episode; (b) **never the `auth` tier** — a fixed `GATEWAY_UNCONFIGURED_COOLDOWN` (60 s) that does not escalate, because escalation's only exit is "fingerprint changed", which structurally cannot exist when there was never a credential; (c) **still not dispatchable** — the reverse guard `test_an_unconfigured_node_is_still_not_dispatched` exists so the fix cannot drift into "unconfigured = healthy", which would make every request pay a doomed provider construction; (d) **self-clears the moment a credential appears**, which is the whole point.
    - **The sentinel that breaks the self-lock, and the discriminator it must never feed.** `note_unconfigured()` writes `{"fingerprint": "absent:never-configured", "kind": "unconfigured"}` — a baseline that is not a credential. Real fingerprints are always `sha:…` / `meta:…`, so the sentinel cannot collide. The trap this design exists to avoid: `note_auth_failure`'s return value is **OPT-027's ban-inference discriminator** ("was this failure on a credential that had already been renewed?"). Recording absent→present as a "renewal" would make a brand-new user's very first real login read as "a renewed credential was still refused" and apply an **auto lease to an account that has never been touched upstream**. The two evidence chains are therefore separate: `renewed_providers()` filters `kind != "unconfigured"`, `note_auth_failure` treats the sentinel as "no previous credential" (`had_credential` false, so `renewed_since_last` stays false), and `unconfigured_providers()` is the only reader. The release path is cause-scoped the same way (`_RELEASABLE_BY_CREDENTIAL_EVENT = _RELEASABLE_BY_RENEWAL + _RELEASABLE_BY_CONFIGURED`), so one trigger can never release the other's cooldown.
    - **An unconfigured release skips probation, deliberately.** OPT-022's serial canary exists for the demand released the instant a ban lifts; a node that has just completed its *first* login has no suppressed backlog, so serializing it and doubling its pacing would punish a healthy new node. Measured after the fix: cooldowns 59.9–60.0 s, `cause=unconfigured`, ledger total 0, `_episode_ends` empty, `credential_watch.is_outage_active() False`, ban-inference evidence `{}`; after a simulated `login.py`, all five released and every cooldown 0.
    - **A new cause is not done until every cause-reader knows it — the `touch_avoidance_reason` cell.** The first version stopped at the cooldown and the dossier, so `touch_avoidance_reason()` still answered the generic `"cooldown"` for an unconfigured node, and every consumer printed the wrong sentence: the probe wrote `风控避让冷却中 … 已跳过真实探针以规避上游封禁`, conversation GC wrote `处于风控避让冷却中`, the healer wrote the same, and `login.py all`'s label branch had no entry. An account that had **never been touched upstream** was described to the operator as a risk-control ban in four places. `touch_avoidance_reason()` now reports `unconfigured` (ordered after `disabled`/`quarantined`, since those are heavier declarations and the "should anything touch this node" answer is the same), and the three consumer label maps gained the honest wording. This also surfaced a **pre-existing** test defect of exactly the [M1] shape: `tests/test_login_sweep.py::test_all_still_logs_in_a_cooling_node` asserted its precondition `reason == "cooldown"` while, on a machine with an empty `session/`, the node is unconfigured — the case was asserting about a window it had not established. It now injects a fake `GLM_AUTH_TOKEN` for the assertion, so the cooling cause really is `ratelimit` and the subject of the test is the subject it names.
    - **The reporting surface had to learn the new cause, in both directions.** `risk_dossier` gained `_SEVERITY_TO_VERDICT["unconfigured"]` (without it the row fell through to the `or "ratelimit"` default and told the operator "no human intervention needed" about a node that needs one command), `_LIVE_ONLY_CAUSES` membership (it never enters the ledger, so the all-time severity would otherwise describe a different, older cooldown), and an `AVOIDANCE_MATRIX` row answering `recoverable_by_relogin: True` — the same answer as `auth`, and deliberately different from `disabled`'s `False`, because "a config declaration" and "a missing credential" have opposite remedies. The ring-exhausted refusal text now names the unconfigured nodes and says 尚未配置/请先登录 instead of 已失效, and `tests/test_risk_dossier.py`'s pinned matrix assertion gained the row with that distinction recorded in the message.
    - **The test's own traps, worth recording because each one would have let the defect pass.** The threshold is `< 600`, not `< 1800`: the escalated cooldown *is* exactly 1800 and reads back as 1799.9 after elapsed time, so `< 1800` is green with the defect fully present (the OPT-031 trap, one tier over). The probation case asserts `"qwen" not in tracker._episode_ends` and **not** `is_in_probation()`, whose first statement returns `False` while a cooldown is live — an assertion about a window the code does not protect ([M1]). The E2E case asserts **state** (ledger count, worst cooldown) rather than the tripwire's construction count, because constructing a provider is purely local and counting it would be the same mistake. And the suite's `run_all()` dispatch named a case that did not exist (`test_end_to_end_a_fresh_clone_refuses_without_touching_upstream`); corrected to the defined `test_end_to_end_a_fresh_clone_pollutes_no_risk_state` rather than by renaming the case.
    - Pinned by `tests/test_unconfigured_credentials.py` (7 cases: 6 RED pre-fix, verified with the measured numbers above, plus the reverse guard that was green before and stays green). Six consecutive runs of five providers is what proves the mechanism, not one: the first failure alone only reaches the 60 s tier.

65. **Web Frontend (`static/index.html`)**:
    - High-aesthetic Codex Light interface with grouped model selector and collapsible ` thinking` drawer.

66. **Codex's Session Identity Lives in `client_metadata` (`providers/anthropic_compat.py:resolve_responses_session_id`, `server.py:responses_endpoint`)**:
    - **[SESSION-ID] The same defect `resolve_client_session_id` fixed for Claude Code (commit `1f8abcf`), one protocol over.** Claude Code's identity was found in `metadata.user_id`; Codex's is in `client_metadata`, and `/v1/responses` read only `req.conversation_id`. Codex 0.145.0 **never** sends that field — it appears **0 times** across the three captured bodies of a real parent→child sub-agent run — and `ResponsesRequest` did not even declare `client_metadata` (`extra="allow"` parked it in `model_extra`, unread). So the entire stickiness / continuation / anonymous-GC chain was inert for every real Codex session: every turn started a **new** upstream conversation and re-sent the full history, and every turn queued the previous live conversation for deletion as if it were an anonymous temporary one. No error at any layer — the agent simply pays more each turn.
    - **The key is `thread_id`, not `session_id`, and that distinction is load-bearing.** Captured from the real binary:
      | body | `client_metadata.thread_id` | `client_metadata.session_id` | `thread_source` |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | parent turn 1 | `…c178…` | `…c178…` | `user` |
      | parent turn 2 | `…c178…` | `…c178…` | `user` |
      | child (spawned sub-agent) | `…c23b…` | `…c178…` (**same**) | `subagent` |
      A sub-agent shares the parent's `session_id` and differs only in `thread_id`. Keying on `session_id` would pin the child to the parent's upstream conversation and let two independent dialogue lines overwrite each other's context — and parent/child concurrency is the *normal* shape of real Codex, not an edge case. `thread_id` is the identifier of "one dialogue line", which is exactly what the affinity key means.
    - **Measured end-to-end with the real binary** (3-round `codex exec`, scripted upstream, `payload_len` = what the provider actually puts on the wire — full history on a fresh conversation, `continuation_content()` when the upstream session is reused):

      | | pre-fix | post-fix |
      |---|---|---|
      | upstream `conversation_id` per turn | `[None, None, None, None]` | `[None, "c1", "c1", "c1"]` |
      | payload chars | 36302 + 36711 + 37120 + 37529 = **147,662** | 36302 + 159 + 159 + 159 = **36,779** |

      A 4x reduction in what the account is charged, growing with every further turn. The offline suite is red on the same defect at turn 2 with `'x-session-affinity': 'fresh', 'x-session-turns': '1'`.
    - **One reading, in one place.** `resolve_responses_session_id()` is now the only judge inside the handler; the nine downstream `req.conversation_id` reads (affinity resolution, `record_turn`, the anonymous-GC guard and the client-facing echo, on both the streaming and non-streaming paths) all read the derived value. Two independent derivations of "is this the same conversation" is precisely the defect shape this file records over and over.
    - **Fail-open on every shape.** An explicit `conversation_id` wins (unambiguous, and what non-Codex clients send); then `thread_id`; then `session_id`; a non-dict `client_metadata`, a missing key or a blank string all return `None`. A `None` key means "this request names no session" — create a new upstream conversation and flatten the full history — which is always correct, merely expensive. Replaying a malformed value as an upstream conversation id would send a *stranger's* context to the model.
    - **The E2E harness now measures it, and its verdict includes it.** `scripts/e2e/scripted_upstream.py` records `conversation_id` and `payload_len` per captured body; `run_agent_loop.py` reports `upstream_convs` / `session_reused` and **fails a multi-round run that never reused a conversation**, in all three modes (single, two-vendor parallel, N sessions of one binary). This was added because the harness previously printed `PASS` for the pre-fix binary run — the loop closed, the tools really executed, and the run was silently 4x more expensive than it needed to be. A harness that cannot see the defect cannot report it, which is §60's lesson one instrument over.
    - Pinned by `tests/test_affinity.py::test_responses_honours_codex_client_metadata_thread_identity` (5 directions: parent turn 1 `fresh`, parent turn 2 `bound` with the upstream conversation carried and **no** deletion, child thread `fresh` with no parent conversation inherited, child turn 2 `bound` on **its own** conversation, parent unaffected afterwards), verified red on the pre-fix code with the exact message `同一 thread_id 的第二轮没有粘住——真实 Codex 从不发 conversation_id`.
    - **The stub had to be made to lie before the assertion could be true.** The first version returned one constant id for every newly created conversation, so parent and child upstream ids were equal by construction and "the child did not inherit the parent's conversation" could not fail. It now returns a distinct id per new conversation, **and the key choice is probed in the other direction**: swapping the resolver's key order to `("session_id", "thread_id")` turns the suite red with `子 agent（不同 thread_id、同一 session_id）被粘到了父线程上`. A test that only goes green on a fix can be green for the wrong reason; this one is red on both mistakes.

67. **The Instrument Must Measure the Wire, Not the Bookkeeping (`scripts/e2e/`)**
    - **[E2E-FIDELITY] Three separate ways the E2E harness was asserting against prompts the upstream never receives.** This suite is the only instrument in the repository that answers the project's central question — *does a real `claude` / `codex` actually work through this gateway?* — so an instrument that quietly disagrees with the thing it measures is worse than no instrument: it reports PASS in proportion to how little it is really checking.
    - **(a) `_flatten` re-implemented the product's flatten.** The stub joined raw text fragments with no role labels, while every provider calls `providers.base.flatten_messages`, which renders `System:` / `User:` / `Assistant:` and appends the ```tool_call``` blocks. So `has_tools_section`, `_declared_tools`, and "did the tool result reach upstream" were all asserted against a prompt the upstream never sees — measured: 186 chars (stub) vs 343 chars (product) for the same message list, with the tool-call block entirely absent from the stub's rendering. It now delegates, so the probe cannot drift from the product.
    - **(b) The tool-result check searched the replayed history instead of the payload.** `round2_saw_result` looked for `Tool Result for` in `prompt_tail`. But a continuation request does **not** send the history — `continuation_content` sends only the new turn (§66) — so the two are different prompts and only one is real. Measured divergence: a round-3 request whose payload is only the new ```tool_call``` still shows `[Tool Result for c1]` in `prompt_full`. The check now finds the newest tool result in the replayed messages and looks for **its text** in the body that actually goes on the wire (`payload_has_tool_result`). Two false-positive traps, both measured: the gateway's own tool contract contains the literal `[Tool Result for <id>]` (round 1 reported `True` before the guard — the same trap §60 records), and a `role:"tool"` message renders as `Tool (name): …` with **no id at all**, so the check keys on text, not on the marker.
    - **(c) The 4th agent turn had no check at all.** `any()` over the bodies let a run that delivers the result at round 2 and loses it at round 4 still report PASS. It now requires **every** round after the first to carry one.
    - **Per-session isolation was unobservable, which is the property the same-binary mode exists for.** The stub answered a literal `"c1"` for every conversation, so "each session reuses its own upstream conversation" and "all sessions share one" produced **byte-identical evidence** — measured: `--sessions 2 --rounds 3` → 8 bodies from 2 sessions, every one `conversation_id="c1"`, printed as PASS. The stub now **mints** a distinct id per conversation (a real upstream does) and logs `served_conv` — the incoming id when continuing, the minted id otherwise — because `conversation_id` alone cannot answer the question (a fresh conversation logs `None`). The counter is module-level on purpose: `LoopProvider` is constructed per request, so a per-instance counter handed every request the same id. The runner now fails when N sessions produce fewer than N distinct served conversations, when more sessions than expected start a fresh conversation (one lost its thread), and when any continuation reaches upstream without its tool result.
    - **The risk-critical number is counted at the upstream, not at the gateway.** The per-node semaphore is *supposed* to bound "one account, several parallel sessions" — the signature an anti-bot system scores — so reading the gateway's own counter is asking the suspect for its alibi (§36 measured peak 6 against a qwen account limited to 3 while the gateway's side looked fine). The stub tracks in-flight/peak per account and exposes them on `GET /e2e/concurrency`; the runner compares the peak against `PROVIDER_RISK_SPECS[...].max_concurrency` **read from the gateway** rather than hardcoding a copy. Measured: 8 concurrent `codex` sessions against one gateway → `{'qwen': 3, 'doubao': 2, 'glm': 2}`, exactly the configured limits. Re-injecting the historical handshake-only release (§36's defect) raises it to `qwen: 4` and fails the run with `qwen: 4 concurrent upstream requests against a limit of 3`. A 0.4 s per-response hold makes the pile-up deliberate: with a fast stub, peak in-flight is timing luck and a leaking semaphore is indistinguishable from a holding one.
    - **The harness improvements are themselves proven able to fail**: withholding the tool result from the wire turns both real binaries red (`!! claude: no captured request carried a tool result`); reverting the stub to a constant conversation id turns the same-binary run red (`2 sessions were served by only 1 upstream conversation(s) — their contexts bleed into each other`); the handshake-release probe turns the concurrency check red. A green verdict from an instrument that cannot go red is a measurement of nothing.

68. **Two Tool Calls Back to Back, and the Second One Disappeared (`providers/anthropic_compat.py:_BLOCK_TOOL_PATTERN`, `scripts/e2e/`)**:
    - **[TOOL-LOOP] The defect is one character of regex, and it silently deleted half an agent turn.** Emitting two tool calls in one assistant turn is normal agent behaviour (read two files, run two commands), and none of the five upstreams has native tool calling — the contract travels as injected prompt text, a ```` ```tool_call ```` fence per call. `_BLOCK_TOOL_PATTERN` closed a fence with `\n?```+`, which is **greedy**: when two fences are written back to back with no separator, the first block's close consumed the *opening* backticks of the second (6 backticks in one match), `finditer` resumed after them, and the second block never matched again. The model's second call reached the client as nothing at all — no error, no partial call, no log line.
    - **Measured, pre-fix, with the exact byte shape real codex 0.145.0 produces** (`TOOL_A` then `TOOL_B`, zero separator): `parse_tool_calls` → `[('Read', {'file_path': 'a'})]` — one call. With a `\n` between the two blocks → both calls. That asymmetry is why it survived: every existing suite separates adjacent blocks with a newline, so the fence shapes it was tested on were exactly the ones that worked. A defect is only invisible if every instrument is pointed somewhere else.
    - **The fix is a lazy close with one negative lookahead, and both halves are load-bearing.** `\`{3,}?` eats three backticks and stops rather than swallowing all of them; `(?!\s*LANG(?![A-Za-z0-9_]))` stops it from halting at a point that is itself the *start* of a new block. The inner `(?![A-Za-z0-9_])` is the second half of the same judgement: `tool_calling` and `json5x` are not language markers, so without it a block ending just before such a language marker would be split where it should not be. The language alternation was extracted to `_TOOL_FENCE_LANG` so the opener and the lookahead cannot drift apart — the `compute_key`/`is_disabled` lesson (one fact, one definition) applied to a regex.
    - **Zero regression was proved, and the first proof was too weak.** The initial claim rested on a hand-written 15-shape matrix (four backticks, every language alias, an unclosed fence, inline prose backticks, newline-separated blocks). A 1029-shape exhaustive sweep (7 openers × 7 separators × 3 closers × affixes) run against the **real** `parse_tool_calls` rather than against the regex found **98 behavioural differences — all of them `1 → 2` calls, all of them separator `""`**, i.e. exactly the intended fix and nothing else. A second pass over the prose residue (what the client receives as text after the calls are removed) found **0 differences** outside the adjacent case. The distinction that mattered: comparing `finditer` groups is not comparing behaviour — 308 group-level differences included **unclosed-first-block** shapes (`\n\n```tool_call\n…` with no closer), where the new close consumes differently but the *parsed output* and the *residue* are byte-identical. Asserting on the regex would have reported a regression that does not exist; asserting on the parser is asserting on the product. `json`/`json5` stay in the whitelist deliberately: real models do emit ```` ```json ```` for calls, and anti-fabrication is enforced by shape (`_extract_single_tool_strict`) and by the declared-name allowlist, not by guessing at formats (section 32).
    - **The assertion lives at the protocol boundary, because the parse function is not the product.** `tests/test_parallel_tool_calls.py` (6 cases) drives `/v1/chat/completions`, `/v1/messages` and `/v1/responses` through a scripted provider and asserts the **client really receives two calls** (two `tool_calls`, two `tool_use` with distinct ids, two `function_call`s with distinct `call_id`s) for all three separators, plus the streaming accumulation path (`feed_text` per chunk — the gap the real client's cadence exposes), plus a three-adjacent-block case that pins the rule (the swallowed block is the one *touching* the previous close, wherever it sits), plus `parallel_tool_calls: false` still trimming to the first — the fix makes two calls visible, so the trim authority must remain the client's declaration and nothing else.
    - **[HARNESS] Three separate instrument defects surfaced while proving the fix, and each one alone would have made a green run meaningless.** (a) The stub counted `[Tool Result for …]` occurrences to decide which round it was in, so a two-call turn jumped the counter 0→2 and the run answered after **one** round instead of driving round two: `rounds=1/2` on a healthy run, because a *round* is an assistant turn, not a result (`results // calls_per_round`). (b) `round2_saw_result` was derived from `bodies[1:]`, which assumes one session's requests are contiguous — with N sessions sharing one log the second session's **first** request (correctly carrying no result) landed in the slice, so a fully healthy run reported `false`; the predicate is now "every request the gateway handed an upstream conversation id to carried its result", which is what a continuation *is* (`_blind_continuations`, shared with the blind-continuation failure so one fact has one definition). (c) The `SECOND_CALL` marker was a hardcoded relative path that only resolves in same-binary mode, and the single- and two-vendor verdicts never checked it at all — so `--two-calls` passed there while proving nothing about the second call. The marker path is now per-mode (`_second_call_marker`) and all three modes assert it.
    - **And the verdict itself could lie.** Each mode recomputed PASS from a hand-picked subset of the facts that had already produced `!!` failure lines, so a run that fired `only the FIRST of two tool calls in one turn executed (SECOND_CALL marker absent)` printed `codex: PASS` directly above it (measured). PASS is now derived from the failure list itself (`_failures_for`): a failure prefixed with another client's name belongs to that client, an unprefixed one is ring-wide and fails every session. This is the same class as OPT-031/035 — *the report asserting something the run did not establish* — and the same cure: one definition, and the verdict reads it rather than paraphrasing it.
    - **Verified with the real binaries, all modes green**: `--rounds 1|2|3` and `--rounds 1 --two-calls` (both clients), `--parallel --rounds 2 --two-calls` (both vendors concurrently), `--parallel --sessions 2|3` (one binary, N sessions). The second call's execution is proved on disk (`SECOND_CALL` in each session's marker directory), and the assertion is proved **able to fail** by forcing the marker check false: `codex: FAIL` + `EXIT: 1`, where the pre-fix verdict printed `PASS` beside the same failure.

69. **A Read Endpoint That Wrote the Repository (`server.py:get_latest_optimization_report`, `healing/task_dispatcher.py:TaskEvaluator`)**:
    - **[PROTOCOL] The symptom was cosmetic and the cause was structural, which is why it survived.** Every full test sweep left `git status` showing three modified files under `reports/` (`optimization_recommendations.json`, `optimization_proposal.json`, `optimization_recommendations.md`), diffing to exactly one changed line: `generated_at`. Content byte-identical, so nothing looked broken — it was dismissed as "the report regenerated". It was not noise to be tolerated; it was a **read** endpoint writing to the repository on every call, and the reason it could do so is that two handlers claimed the same path.
    - **Two registrations, one path, and the loser is invisible.** `GET /v1/reports/latest` was declared on `get_tasks_evaluation` (server.py:1495) *and* on `get_latest_optimization_report` (server.py:2046). FastAPI dispatches to the **first** match, so the second one — whose docstring reads "优先从 `reports/optimization_proposal.json` 读取，不存在则动态生成", i.e. exactly the read-only contract that was wanted — was unreachable dead code for its entire life. The frontend (`static/index.html`) already treats `/v1/reports/latest` as a **read** with `/v1/tasks/evaluation` as its fallback, so the intended design was in the codebase the whole time, just not in the dispatch table. Verified by enumerating `app.routes`: both paths appeared twice, first-`endpoint` = `get_tasks_evaluation`.
    - **What the read path was actually doing, measured**: `TaskEvaluator.evaluate_optimization_opportunities()` is pure dict assembly (**4.11 ms median**, n=20) followed by three synchronous file writes — executed *inline in an async handler*, so every read request blocked the event loop for the full duration. It never persisted anything meaningful either: both call sites pass no `tasks`, so the report body is a function of code constants alone and is identical on every call. The only thing that changed was the timestamp it stamped into files under version control.
    - **The fix is not "don't write there", it is to restore the two endpoints' identities.** `/v1/tasks/evaluation` is an **action** (aggregate tasks, persist a fresh report) and keeps `get_tasks_evaluation`; `/v1/reports/latest` is a **read** and now dispatches to `get_latest_optimization_report`, which serves the stored snapshot verbatim and, only when no snapshot exists, generates one with `persist=False` **in a worker thread** (`anyio.to_thread.run_sync`, max observed event-loop gap 4.7 ms against 41 ms of synchronous work). The two registrations are gone, which is what makes the handler reachable at all.
    - **Two latent defects had to be fixed for the isolation to be real, and both are the OPT-019 shape one system over.** (a) `TaskEvaluator.__init__` accepted `project_root` and then never used it for persisting — the save path read the module-global `REPORTS_DIR`, so `TaskEvaluator(project_root=tmp)` still wrote to the developer's checkout and only looked isolated because every caller happened to run inside `patch("healing.task_dispatcher.REPORTS_DIR", …)`. The instance now resolves `self.reports_dir` from its own root, and `TaskDispatcher` passes its **raw** argument down rather than a pre-resolved `PROJECT_ROOT` (pre-resolving makes "caller injected nothing" indistinguishable from "caller injected this exact directory", which silently defeats the test-temp-dir branch — measured: the first version of that branch never fired). (b) `tests/test_dispatcher.py` never set `TESTING`, so its integration case drove the real action endpoint and dirtied three tracked files per run; it now sets `TESTING=1` per the established convention, and a `TESTING=1` evaluator with no injected root writes to `tempfile.mkdtemp()` instead of the repository.
    - **One case in the new suite lied before it was tightened, and that is the reusable part.** `test_absent_snapshot_is_generated_once_not_persisted` pointed `srv._REPORTS_DIR` at an empty temp dir and asserted nothing was written there — which **passed on the pre-fix code**, because the old handler ignored `_REPORTS_DIR` on that branch and wrote to the real repository instead. It was asserting the wrong surface: "not written here" is not "not written". It now also fingerprints the repository before and after, and is red pre-fix for the right reason (`快照缺失时读取接口改写到了别处（真实仓库）`). The general rule is the one already in this file repeatedly: an assertion has to be pointed at the surface that actually carries the behaviour.
    - **All six cases were verified RED on the pre-fix tree** (`git worktree add /tmp/rep_base HEAD`), each with its own measured message rather than a shared import error: repository rewrite, stale `generated_at` (`'2026-10-24 17:22:02' != '2026-10-24 17:21:52'`), the leak above, the missing off-loop helper, the duplicate registration (`/reports/latest ('GET',) -> ['get_tasks_evaluation', 'get_latest_optimization_report']`, and `/v1/reports/latest` likewise), and the decorative `project_root` (`落盘到了 []`). Full sweep: **76 files, 0 failures**, and the working tree is clean afterwards — the check that started all of this.
    - **The seventh case went through three designs, and the two discarded ones are the lesson.** The first was a *forward-looking* static guard: an AST scan of each GET handler's own body for write calls. It **passed on the pre-fix tree**, because the write was not in the handler — it was two calls down. "A red-first suite" was therefore false for that case, and rather than describe it as a regression guard I widened it: (b) resolve the whole call graph by name and flag any reachable function that writes. That version failed on the **fixed** tree, reporting `/v1/admin/dispatch-audit` and `/v1/admin/ban-inference` as writers — a **false positive**: name-only resolution matched Python's `dict.get()` to `DeviceIdentityStore.get()`, a real mint-once method that does persist (by design, and unreachable from those routes). Both failures are the same mistake in different directions: static reachability is an *approximation* of what code does, so it produces false negatives when the depth exceeds what you walked and false positives whenever two unrelated things share a name.
    - **The design that works observes the behaviour instead of modelling it.** `test_no_get_route_writes_to_disk_at_runtime` patches the write primitives themselves (`io.open` / `builtins.open` in write modes, `json.dump`, `os.replace`, `Path.mkdir` / `write_text` / `write_bytes`), re-requests every GET route on the real app, and fails on the first write, printing the exact call chain. Depth is irrelevant to it, and it names no functions, so nothing can be confused by a shared name. Verified RED on the pre-fix tree with a message that identifies the whole hidden path — and it immediately earned its keep by finding a **second, unfixed instance of the same defect on the fixed tree** (`GET /v1/tasks/evaluation`, below).
    - **The second instance it found, and the general rule it establishes.** `/v1/tasks/evaluation` — the sibling of the endpoint that started all of this — is registered as **GET** and called `task_dispatcher.get_latest_evaluation_report()`, which called the evaluator with the default `persist=True`: three file writes plus a `mkdir` per read, silently. The report's content is a pure function of code constants (`tasks` is accepted and **never read**), so "the report changed" and "a task changed state" are the same event, and persistence belongs on the **state transition**, not on the read. Persistence now happens at the two terminal transitions (`completed` / `failed`) via the new `TaskDispatcher.persist_evaluation_report()`; `get_latest_evaluation_report()` is `persist=False`, and the `mkdir` moved inside the `if persist:` guard because creating a directory is a write side effect too. Reading still regenerates from live memory rather than returning a possibly-stale on-disk snapshot (after a restart `self.tasks` is empty and the file describes another process), which is why "read does not persist" and "read is not stale" are compatible rather than contradictory.
    - The general form now holds across all 28 parameterless GET routes: **a read may compute, but it may not touch the disk** — and the check for that is a tripwire on the write primitives, not a reading of the call graph.
70. **Tool Descriptions Are a Contract, Not a Label (`providers/anthropic_compat.py:format_tools_system_prompt`)**:
    - **[TOOL-LOOP] The gateway threw away the sentence that tells the model how not to fail.** Every tool description was rendered as `description.strip().split("\n")[0][:200]` — first line, 200 characters. That is correct only if the first line is the whole description. Real client declarations open with a heading (`# Edit`, `# SendMessage`) and put the *preconditions* on the following lines, so what reached the model was a title and nothing else. Measured on the real 20-tool Claude Code capture (`tests/fixtures/claude_code_20_tools.json`): **SendMessage 4259 → 13 chars, Bash 1096 → 47, Edit 360 → 44**, and the total description text shipped was **2078 of 28195 characters — a 13.57× loss**.
    - **Why this is a correctness bug and not formatting**: the five upstreams have no native tool calling, so the prompt is the *only* contract the model ever sees, and the gateway performs **no parameter validation** — a call that violates a precondition is executed anyway and fails on the client. The sentences lost were exactly those preconditions: Edit's "You must Read the file in this conversation before editing, or the call will fail", Read's "must be an absolute path", Write's "Overwriting an existing file you haven't Read will fail", Bash's "Working directory persists between". Measured on the pre-fix tree: all four absent from the prompt (`prompt chars: 15057`); after the fix all four present (`25129`). The model was being told a tool exists and then not told how to use it — the same "declared capability never honoured" class as sections 39/62, pointed at the model instead of at the client.
    - **Truncation is now a budget decision, not a constant.** Descriptions are whitespace-normalized (newlines and markdown table rows become `; `-separated prose, because a web-chat model reads a flat paragraph better than a table) and capped by the same **ladder** discipline the schema path already uses: `_TOOL_DESC_LADDER = (None, 2000, 800, 400, 160, 40)`, and the first tier whose *whole-prompt* description total fits `_TOOL_DESC_BUDGET` (20000) is chosen. The ladder is applied **uniformly to every tool** — never by array position — so tool capability cannot depend on where a client happened to declare it (the same rule as the schema tier). For the 20-tool capture that selects the 800 tier (full 28195 > 20000; 2000 gives 21013 > 20000; 800 gives 12150, fits), so 9 tools are capped and the full prompt is 25129 chars.
    - **A capped description must say it is capped.** `_cap_tool_description` appends `…(+N chars)` rather than ending mid-sentence, because a model that cannot tell a truncated description from a complete one will treat the cut point as the end of the contract — the same "a truncated answer must not impersonate a complete one" reasoning as section 52, one layer earlier. Pinned by `test_description_truncation_is_announced_not_silent`.
    - **The case reads the real capture, not a hand-written sample.** A hand-written tool would only prove "the shape I invented survives"; the defect lives in the shape real clients emit. `test_tool_preconditions_survive_into_the_prompt` loads the fixture, asserts four specific precondition sentences are present, and asserts the prompt exceeds 20000 chars — measured red on the pre-fix tree with all four missing.

71. **Records of Server-Side Actions Are Not User Speech (`providers/responses_compat.py:_server_side_action_text`, `providers/anthropic_compat.py:extract_anthropic_content`)**:
    - **[TOOL-LOOP] Six item types the model was told the *user* had said.** Section 43 fixed the Responses input converter's fallback branch for `reasoning`, which Codex replays verbatim; the branch itself remained, and the audit measured it still leaking every other server-executed item type: `web_search_call`, `file_search_call`, `computer_call`, `mcp_call`, `image_generation_call`, `code_interpreter_call`. Each fell through to the generic path, was serialized to raw JSON, and was appended as a **`user`** message. Measured on the pre-fix tree, all six: `web_search_call -> user '{"type": "web_search_call", "id": "s1", "status": "completed", "action…`.
    - **Two wrong readings from one rendering.** A record of an action the model itself initiated, that the server executed, presented as user speech tells the model the user said something they never said (context pollution it will reason from) *and* hides that the action actually happened — so the model may repeat the identical search it can no longer see. It is the client's own output replayed verbatim, which is why this is history fidelity rather than a display choice.
    - **They now render as assistant records carrying the action detail**: `[Server action: web search — claude docs]`, with the detail pulled from `action.query` / `command` / `name` / `tool` / … in that order (falling back to the item's own top-level fields, then to a stated absence: "was performed by the server; its payload is not available"). The action *content* is what makes the record useful — a bare "a search happened" still invites the repeat — so the case asserts the query string is present, not merely the role. A non-`completed` status is appended, since a failed search is not the same history as a successful one.
    - **The routing rule is by declaration, not by guessing**: `_SERVER_SIDE_ITEM_TYPES` plus any `*_call` suffix not already claimed by a specific handler. The suffix branch needed an explicit exclusion tuple — `function_call`, `custom_tool_call`, `shell_call` have their own handlers and were being swallowed by the generic rule (`test_both_history_shapes_render_identically` caught it). `local_shell_call_output` was likewise removed from the set for the same reason: it is an *output*, and its dedicated branch must win.
    - **The other direction of the same defect: silence.** Anything genuinely unknown now gets a typed placeholder (`[Client item omitted: <type> — this gateway cannot render it]`) instead of raw JSON. Silence is worse than a placeholder because a model that does not see content concludes it does not exist, then answers as though nothing was missing. The Anthropic block extractor had exactly that shape — `server_tool_use` and `web_search_tool_result` blocks simply vanished (measured pre-fix: `extract_anthropic_content([...]) == 'before'`) — and now announces them too.
    - **One case in the suite passed on the pre-fix tree on first write, and the reason is reusable.** `test_truly_unknown_items_are_announced_not_dumped` searched `json.dumps(msgs)` for `'"secret"'`; inside a nested string the quotes are escaped to `\"secret\"`, so the needle could never match and the assertion was true by construction — a green test measuring nothing. It now scans the message **bodies**, and was verified red on baseline with the real leaked payload visible. This is section 69's "an assertion has to be pointed at the surface that actually carries the behaviour" arriving through a serialization boundary rather than a call graph.

72. **Unparseable Tool Arguments Are Not "No Arguments" (`providers/base.py:_serialize_tool_calls`)**:
    - **[TOOL-LOOP] `{}` is an assertion, and it was the wrong one.** When a replayed assistant `tool_calls` entry carried an `arguments` string that was not valid JSON (or a non-dict value), `flatten_messages` degraded it to `{}` — measured pre-fix: the literal `/tmp/a.py` from the damaged argument appeared **nowhere** in the prompt, while `"arguments": {}` did. `{}` tells the model "this call passed no arguments", which is a claim about the client's behaviour; the truth was "we could not parse what the client sent", which is a claim about ours. A model handed the first story re-issues the call from scratch, or cannot explain the `[Tool Result for c1]` that follows it.
    - **The Responses path already had it right, which is what made this a defect rather than a design.** `responses_compat._tool_call_block` has always preserved the raw text as `{"_raw": ...}`. Two rendering rules for one fact is the recurring shape in this file (sections 43, 62), so `base.py` now matches: unparseable text and non-dict values become `{"_raw": <original>}`. Empty or whitespace-only `arguments` still means genuinely no arguments and is **not** wrapped — the distinction is content, not absence.
    - **The case pins both paths in one place.** `test_unparseable_arguments_are_preserved_not_emptied` asserts `_raw` and the original literal survive through `flatten_messages` **and** through `_tool_call_block`, so the two cannot drift apart again, and asserts the empty-string case stays unwrapped so the fix does not over-apply. Verified red on the pre-fix tree with `损坏的参数被静默清成了 {}: User: go`.

73. **A Server-Held History Id Must Be Refused, Not Silently Ignored (`server.py:responses_endpoint`, `providers/responses_compat.py:ResponsesRequest`)**:
    - **[PROTOCOL] The same "declared, parsed, read by nobody" shape as sections 34b/35, one field over.** The Responses protocol lets a client send only the **delta** and leave the prefix to the server: `{"previous_response_id": "resp_...", "input": "<this turn only>"}`. The field was not declared on `ResponsesRequest`, so `extra="allow"` swept it into `model_extra` and no code ever looked at it again. Measured on the pre-fix tree against a scripted provider (`TESTING=1`, zero real upstream calls):
      ```
      body: {"model":"gpt-4o","store":true,"previous_response_id":"resp_abc123",
             "input":[{"type":"message","role":"user","content":"what did I ask before?"}]}
      -> HTTP 200, upstream prompt exactly:
         [{"role": "user", "content": "what did I ask before?"}]
      ```
      The model receives a question with no antecedent and answers it confidently. A wrong answer with no error, on the one field whose entire contract is "the server supplies the context".
    - **The gateway cannot honour it, and that is a fact about the product, not a missing feature**: nothing persists response objects, so *any* `previous_response_id` is necessarily unresolvable. That leaves two honest options — refuse, or start a fresh session — and only one of them is honest. Starting fresh is precisely the behaviour measured above.
    - **Refuse with the code clients already have recovery logic for.** Real SDKs and gateways key on OpenAI's own `404` + `code: previous_response_not_found` to fall back to resending the full history. Inventing a different code (or answering 400) leaves those clients on a path nobody wrote. The message names the field and the remedy, because an operator reading it needs to know the fix is "send the whole history", not "retry".
    - **Zero upstream, on both transports**: the check sits at the top of `responses_endpoint`, before any provider is constructed, and the suite asserts `not CALLS` for `stream=False` **and** `stream=True` — this is the H4 rule (a deterministic zero-upstream refusal belongs on the status line, as `application/json`, not as a 200 carrying an in-band SSE error a client would try to parse as a stream).
    - **Zero regression is asserted, not assumed**: a second case posts a request without the field and pins `200`, exactly one upstream call and a `message` output item, so the refusal cannot grow into "reject anything that looks like continuation".
    - The field is now **declared** on the model rather than left to `extra="allow"`, so the next reader can see it; the refusal itself lives in `server.py` so it precedes dispatch and composes with the other pre-dispatch gates. Pinned by `tests/test_responses_previous_id.py` (2 cases), verified red on a pristine `77ee011` worktree with `previous_response_id 被静默忽略了（HTTP 200）`.

74. **An Upstream Conversation Id Is Node-Private (`providers/failover.py:_kwargs_for_candidate`, `server.py:_affinity_record` / `_serving_provider`)**:
    - **[TOOL-LOOP] The rule existed, and it had only one door.** `resolve_affinity`'s handoff branch strips the heterogeneous upstream id when a session migrates vendors (M3/OPT-007). Transparent failover is the **second** path that moves a request to another vendor, and it never goes through that branch: `server.py` wrote `affinity_decision.upstream_conv_id` into `chat_args` once, and `providers/failover.py` handed it to every candidate via `call_kwargs = dict(chat_args)`. Measured with a session bound to glm, glm returning 429, and the ring failing over to doubao: `doubao received conversation_id="glm-conv"`.
    - **The weight is not the string, it is the branch it triggers.** All five providers open with `prompt = continuation_content(messages) if conversation_id else flatten_messages(messages)`. A non-empty id means "continue this conversation", so the target sends only the last turn's delta — measured **18 characters against an 83-character history** — and answers a conversation it cannot possibly continue. The id also belongs to a different vendor's conversation space, which the target neither recognises nor can resume. A confident wrong answer, no error anywhere.
    - **One failover turn poisons the binding permanently.** `record_turn` was called with the pre-failover provider (the bind node) and the *serving* node's id, so the registry became `provider_key='glm', upstream_conv_id='doubao-conv'` (measured). `SessionAffinityManager` overwrites `upstream_conv_id` only on truthy values and exposes no clear API, so when glm recovered, every subsequent turn of that session was fed a foreign id, and nothing could ever correct it. The defect is one line of state, and it is self-perpetuating.
    - **The fix is the node-privacy rule, applied at both doors.** `FailoverRouter._kwargs_for_candidate` returns the args unchanged for the primary (value-for-value) and `conversation_id=None` for any other candidate, so a redirect opens a fresh upstream conversation and flattens the full history — which is exactly what the handoff branch already decided. `_serving_provider` picks the node that *actually* answered (`failover_event.to_provider`, not the pre-failover primary), and `_affinity_record` records the observed id **only when the serving node is the bound node**; otherwise it records `None`, meaning "the gateway holds no replayable upstream session for this binding", so the next request opens one and flattens. All eight record sites (three protocols × streaming/non-streaming) go through the pair, so the rule has one definition rather than eight chances to drift.
    - **`degraded` keeps its meaning**: a short cooldown still retains the binding (M3), and now also correctly declines to write the other node's id under it — the previous code already passed `None` for that one case, which is how the same rule was known and applied in exactly one of the two situations that needed it.
    - Pinned by `tests/test_session_continuity.py` (2 cases), verified red on a pristine `7d47658` worktree with both measured symptoms: `改派到 [doubao] 时仍带着原节点的上游会话 id 'glm-conv'` and `原绑定节点 [glm] 的记录里被写进了改派节点 [doubao] 自己的上游会话 id`.

75. **Session Identity Belongs in the Dedup Key (`providers/singleflight.py:compute_key`)**:
    - **[SINGLEFLIGHT] The same one-line rule as section 53, applied to the fact that decides what counts as "context".** A dedup key's only correctness criterion is *identical fingerprint ⇒ the two requests' correct answers are interchangeable*. `compute_key` covered provider / model / messages / tools / temperature / thinking / stream / constraints — but not **which conversation this is**, and that is precisely what decides where the prefix lives: a bound session continues upstream and sends only the delta; a fresh one flattens the whole history into the prompt. Two requests can have byte-identical `messages` and completely different correct answers.
    - **Measured on the real ASGI path** (scripted provider, `TESTING=1`, zero upstream calls): session A bound to upstream `up-1`, session B fresh, identical `messages` ⇒ identical fingerprint ⇒ B became a follower and was handed A's continuation answer (`CONT-ANSWER`) from a **single** upstream call; B's registry entry was never created, yet its response carried `x-session-affinity: bound` — the gateway asserting a binding that does not exist.
    - **This is the ordinary agent-topology case, not a corner.** A Codex or Claude Code sub-agent sends the same instruction text and declares the same tools as its parent, so the fingerprint matches while the child's own conversation has not been created yet. The child is served the parent's continuation — the most reachable form of "the sub-agent answered something nobody asked it".
    - **Adding the fields is the only safe direction, and the opposite error is asserted too.** `session_key` / `upstream_conv_id` are new optional parameters; anonymous callers pass `None` for both, so their payload is value-for-value unchanged (`None` → `""`) and genuinely interchangeable requests still dedup — pinned by a second case, because a key so specific that nothing ever dedups would be a worse regression than the original defect (section 53's rule, now applied twice).
    - Wired at all three protocol call sites rather than only widening the signature: widening it alone passes every direct-call unit test while the real request path still drops the field — the shape section 53 recorded for `constraints`.
    - Pinned by `tests/test_singleflight_session_identity.py` (2 cases), verified red on a pristine `7d47658` worktree with `已绑定会话 A 与全新会话 B 的请求被合并成了一次上游调用（实测 1 次）`.

76. **Continuation May Omit History, Never the Standing Contract (`providers/base.py:continuation_content`)**:
    - **[SESSION-CONTINUITY] The same "declared then dropped" shape, but this one only fails from turn two onward.** Every provider opens with `prompt = continuation_content(messages) if conversation_id else flatten_messages(messages)`, and `continuation_content` returned everything **after the last user message** — which on a bound session means the system block is gone. Measured on the real HTTP path (scripted provider, `TESTING=1`, zero upstream calls):

      ```
      turn 1  prompt = "System: You are Claude Code. …\n\n## Tools Available\n…\n\nUser: q1"   (1069 chars)
      turn 2  prompt = "q2"                                                                    (2 chars)
      ```

      On turn two the upstream received `## Tools Available` = False, `Bash` = False, `System:` = False, `STOP` = False.
    - **Three separate consequences, none of them an error**: the model no longer knows which tools it has; tools declared **after** turn one (MCP mounted mid-session, a sub-agent's own set) never appear at all; and per-turn constraints injected into the system block — `tool_choice:"none"`, `response_format` — vanish exactly on the turns after the client asked for them. For an agent client this is the worst shape there is: turn one works, then "the model suddenly stopped calling tools", with HTTP 200, `finish_reason:"stop"` and perfectly normal-looking content.
    - **History and standing contract are different things, and only history is optional.** The upstream conversation already holds the history — that is what `continuation_content` is for. It does not hold the system block's *later* versions: Claude Code sends a fresh one every turn (measured 8662 chars vs 8594+119 chars across two turns), and the gateway has no way to know which version upstream stored. So the current version is sent every turn. The cost is one repeated system block; the alternative has no compensation mechanism at all.
    - **Contract before the delta, matching turn one's order.** `flatten_messages` renders `System:` first and the turn's input after it, so `continuation_content` does the same — the model's view of the request does not change shape between "first turn" and "continuation". Only `system` messages **before** the last user turn are hoisted; any system message already in the tail renders normally and is not duplicated.
    - **Zero regression, asserted in both directions**: with no system message the result is value-for-value the old one (`"今天天气如何"`), and the tool-loop case from section 32 still delivers the tool result and the call context. `tests/test_tool_roundtrip.py`'s `test_continuation_without_tools_is_unchanged` had pinned the defect as the contract (`== "今天天气如何"`, i.e. the system block dropped) — it was **corrected**, with a comment recording what it had been protecting.
    - Pinned by `tests/test_continuation_contract.py` (7 cases: 4 prompt-layer, 3 through the real `/v1/chat/completions` path asserting on the prompt the provider actually received), verified red on the pre-fix tree with `续聊增量里没有系统提示，模型从第二轮起就读不到常驻指令了。实测: 'q2'`.

77. **A Missing `type` Is Not a Malformed Item (`providers/responses_compat.py:convert_responses_to_gateway_messages`)**:
    - **[PROTOCOL] The most ordinary request shape there is, answered with a 500.** `{"role": "user", "content": "hi"}` — no `type` — is what the OpenAI SDK's `EasyInputMessage` sends and what hand-written clients send. `item.get("type")` returned `None`, the next line called `item_type.endswith("_call")`, and the request died with `AttributeError: 'NoneType' object has no attribute 'endswith'`, returned to the client as an internal server error carrying a traceback.
    - **OpenAI's own definition settles the semantics**: an input item that has a `role` *is* a message. So the missing `type` is normalised to `"message"` before dispatch, and an item with neither `type` nor `role` keeps the pre-existing unknown-item path (announced, not dumped — section 43's rule).
    - **One rule, not two**: `test_typed_and_untyped_messages_render_identically` asserts a typed and an untyped `message` with identical content produce **identical** gateway messages, which is the assertion that stops the normalisation from becoming a second rendering path. Verified red on the pre-fix tree with the 500 (`不带 type 的 SDK 形状被拒绝为 500`).

78. **An Unconfigured Node Must Be a Hard Block at the Dispatch Boundary, Not a Demoted Candidate (`providers/failover.py:evaluate_dispatch_gate`, `providers/risk_dossier.py:derive_posture`, `server.py:health`)**:
    - **[READINESS] The day-zero state, and four readiness surfaces that all described a gateway that could not serve.** On this machine `session/` holds only `.gitkeep` — no credentials at all. Measured before the fix: `GET /health` → `status: ok` (**degraded**, then, only if you read `providers[*].status` and noticed all five said `offline`); `GET /v1/admin/dispatch-audit` → `predicted_primary_dispatchable: true`, 5 of 5; `GET /v1/admin/risk` → every posture `clear`, `dispatchable: true`; and a real `POST /v1/chat/completions` → five local `ProviderError(401)` — the gateway walked the whole ring, constructed a provider per node, and failed five times, all in-process. The gate had exactly four branches (`credential_outage` → `disabled` → `quarantine` → `long_avoidance`) and none of them asked the most basic question there is: **does this deployment hold a credential for this node at all?**
    - **Why "no credential" is a hard block and not the demote-only semantics the gate gives short coolings**: OPT-021 deliberately keeps short coolings demote-only so a ring where everything is briefly cooling can still make a last-resort attempt — a cooldown is a window that expires on its own. A missing credential is not a window; it waits on a human running `login.py`, and a dispatch attempt against it can only produce a **local** failure with zero upstream calls and zero information. Walking the ring for it costs the client one doomed construction per node and, worse, tells every readiness surface afterwards that the node was "tried".
    - **A fifth gate branch, placed LAST**: `blocked_by="unconfigured"`, `cause="unconfigured"`. Last, because the other four are heavier declarations (a lease or an operator disable must be reported first — the danger-first ordering established in OPT-035), and because a node that is *both* banned and unconfigured must keep its `ban` advice: "just log in" is exactly the wrong instruction for a banned account. `remaining_seconds` reports a live cooldown only, never a fabricated countdown (the `disabled` rule), and the refusal is `503 / err_type="unconfigured"` so the H6 `x-should-retry: false` + `Retry-After` headers attach (`_is_deterministic_refusal` answers `False` below 500).
    - **The judgement is a fact, not a message match**: `session_store.is_configured(provider_key)` — "does the resolver return a session with a token right now" — which is verbatim the condition `server.py:_make_provider` raises on and `healing/monitor.py` already judges. Sniffing `"未检测到 … 令牌"` out of the error text would have been easier and would let a re-worded message decide whether a node is dispatchable. Unknown key → `True`; resolver exception → `True`: **判定失败必须退回旧行为**, because misreading a configured node as unconfigured would let a real upstream 401 escape auth avoidance.
    - **One predicate, four consumers, and the drift that was caught in the middle of the work**: the gate, `get_fallback_candidates`, `risk_dossier.derive_posture` (new `unconfigured` posture between `disabled` and `long_avoidance`, `POSTURE_DISPATCHABLE=False`) and `/health`/`/v1/diagnostics` all read the same function. The first version guarded the switch in `evaluate_dispatch_gate` only, not in the candidate filter — measured: `GATEWAY_DISABLE_UNCONFIGURED_GATE=1` left `gate(qwen) → dispatchable True` but `get_fallback_candidates("qwen","Qwen") → []`, i.e. the kill switch **reported itself off while the behaviour stayed on**. Both sites now ask `_unconfigured_gate_disabled()`, and re-measured: switch on → gate dispatchable, candidates `['glm','doubao','kimi','deepseek']`.
    - **The readiness surface is derived from the dispatch gate, not from a second opinion**: `providers[*].dispatchable` is `configured and evaluate_dispatch_gate(pk)["dispatchable"]`, and `/health`'s `status` is `ok` iff at least one node is dispatchable, else `degraded` **with** a `status_reason` naming the remedy. This is OPT-032/033/035's defect class on the surface with the widest audience — every operator, monitoring probe and agent setup script reads `/health` first, and `ok` is an instruction to start sending requests.
    - **A sibling honesty defect found by running the fix's own kill-switch case**: the ring-exhausted report appended "握手总预算 180s 已按剩余候选公平分摊……请调大 `GATEWAY_HANDSHAKE_BUDGET`" whenever the budget was *enabled*, even when every failure was an instant local 401 and no attempt had ever been capped — pointing the operator at a knob that had no bearing on the request. `[BUDGET-2]` now tracks `_budget_was_capped` across the attempt loop and the note is conditional on it.
